#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
scripts/verify_manifest.py - Cryptographic Artifact Integrity Verifier
Verificador Criptográfico de Integridade de Artefatos

Validates registered SHA-256 hashes in data/manifest_sha256.json against files on disk.
Valida os hashes SHA-256 registrados em data/manifest_sha256.json contra os arquivos em disco.

Author / Autor: Rafael Paniccia Cassetta (UFABC)
Language / Idioma: Bilingual (PT-BR & EN-US)
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import hashlib
import json
import os
import sys

REPO_DIR = os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)))
MANIFEST_PATH = os.path.join(REPO_DIR, "data", "manifest_sha256.json")


def compute_sha256(filepath: str) -> str:
    """Compute SHA-256 hex digest of a file in 64 KB streaming chunks."""
    h = hashlib.sha256()
    with open(filepath, "rb") as f:
        while chunk := f.read(65536):
            h.update(chunk)
    return h.hexdigest()


def update_manifest() -> int:
    """Recomputes SHA-256 for all tracked files and updates data/manifest_sha256.json."""
    if not os.path.exists(MANIFEST_PATH):
        print(f"❌ [ERROR] Manifest file not found: {MANIFEST_PATH}")
        return 1

    with open(MANIFEST_PATH, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        manifest = json.load(f)

    # Also track additional core files if present
    additional = [
        "benchmark/adapters/bun_minimal_adapter.py",
        "scripts/run_prefill_ablation.py",
        "scripts/run_w2_resample.py",
        "scripts/run_warm_cache_campaign.py",
        "results/exploratory_campaigns/raw_trials_pre_w2_fix.jsonl",
        "results/publication_campaign/prefill_regression_fit.json",
        "results/publication_campaign/tables/table_prefill_ablation.tex",
        "results/publication_campaign/tables/table_warm_cache.tex",
        "results/publication_campaign/warm_cache_trials.jsonl",
        "results/publication_campaign/warm_cache_summary.json",
        "results/publication_campaign/warm_cache_statistical_tests.json",
        "results/publication_campaign/campaign_metadata.json",
        "results/publication_campaign/spearman_correlation_matrix.json",
        "results/publication_campaign/tables/table_spearman_correlation.tex",
        "results/publication_campaign/prefill_ablation.jsonl",
    ]
    for rel_path in additional:
        abs_path = os.path.join(REPO_DIR, rel_path)
        if os.path.exists(abs_path) and rel_path not in manifest:
            manifest[rel_path] = ""

    updated = {}
    for rel_path in sorted(manifest.keys()):
        if rel_path.startswith("results/simulated_check/"):
            continue
        abs_path = os.path.join(REPO_DIR, rel_path)
        if os.path.exists(abs_path):
            updated[rel_path] = compute_sha256(abs_path)
        else:
            print(f"⚠️  [SKIP] File missing on disk, omitting from updated manifest: {rel_path}")

    with open(MANIFEST_PATH, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        json.dump(updated, f, indent=2)
        f.write("\n")

    print(f"✅ [SUCCESS] Updated {len(updated)} tracked file signatures in {MANIFEST_PATH}")
    return 0


def verify_manifest() -> int:
    if "--update" in sys.argv:
        return update_manifest()

    if not os.path.exists(MANIFEST_PATH):
        print(f"❌ [ERROR] Manifest file not found: {MANIFEST_PATH}")
        return 1

    with open(MANIFEST_PATH, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        manifest = json.load(f)

    total_files = len(manifest)
    print("========================================================================")
    print(" 🔐 [PT-BR] Verificação de Integridade Criptográfica (SHA-256)")
    print(f"    [EN-US] Cryptographic Artifact Integrity Verification ({total_files} files)")
    print("========================================================================")

    mismatches = []
    missing = []
    verified_count = 0

    for rel_path, exp_val in manifest.items():
        abs_path = os.path.join(REPO_DIR, rel_path)
        if not os.path.exists(abs_path):
            missing.append(rel_path)
            continue

        if isinstance(exp_val, dict):
            expected_hash = exp_val.get("sha256", "")
        else:
            expected_hash = str(exp_val)

        actual_hash = compute_sha256(abs_path)
        if actual_hash.lower() != expected_hash.lower():
            mismatches.append((rel_path, expected_hash, actual_hash))
        else:
            verified_count += 1

    if missing:
        print(f"\n❌ [ERROR] Missing files ({len(missing)}):")
        for m in missing:
            print(f"   - {m}")

    if mismatches:
        print(f"\n❌ [ERROR] Hash mismatches ({len(mismatches)}):")
        for p, exp, act in mismatches:
            print(f"   - {p}")
            print(f"     Expected: {exp}")
            print(f"     Actual:   {act}")

    if not missing and not mismatches:
        print(f"✅ [SUCCESS] All {verified_count}/{total_files} tracked files match their cryptographic SHA-256 signature!")
        print("   [PT-BR] Todos os arquivos rastreados conferem com suas assinaturas SHA-256 registradas.")
        print("========================================================================\n")
        return 0
    else:
        print(f"\n⚠️  [FAILURE] Verification failed: {verified_count}/{total_files} valid, {len(mismatches)} altered, {len(missing)} missing.")
        print("========================================================================\n")
        return 1


if __name__ == "__main__":
    sys.exit(verify_manifest())

"""
Unit Tests for Adapter Registry & Fail-Fast Architecture
Testes Unitários para Registro de Adaptadores e Arquitetura Fail-Fast

Author / Autor: Rafael Paniccia Cassetta (UFABC)
Language / Idioma: Bilingual (PT-BR & EN-US comments, English code)
"""

import os

import pytest

from benchmark.adapters import ADAPTER_REGISTRY, get_adapter
from benchmark.adapters.base import AgentOrchestratorAdapter
from benchmark.run_unified_benchmark import (
    check_server_health,
    run_campaign,
    start_mock_server_if_needed,
)

REPO_DIR = os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)))


def test_adapter_registry_contains_all_registered_agents():
    """Verify all 6 evaluation targets including bun_minimal are registered in ADAPTER_REGISTRY."""
    expected_agents = {
        "picoclaw",
        "openclaw_minimal",
        "openclaw_full",
        "python_native",
        "neural_baseline",
        "bun_minimal",
    }
    registered = set(ADAPTER_REGISTRY.keys())
    assert expected_agents.issubset(registered), f"Missing agents: {expected_agents - registered}"


def test_bun_minimal_adapter_metadata_and_execution():
    """Verify bun_minimal adapter properly exposes runner metadata and conforms to interface."""
    adapter = get_adapter("bun_minimal", REPO_DIR)
    meta = adapter.metadata()
    assert "runner_binary_used" in meta
    assert "is_fallback_node" in meta
    assert isinstance(meta["is_fallback_node"], bool)
    assert meta["mode"] == "minimal"


def test_adapter_instances_subclass_base_adapter():
    """Verify every registered adapter conforms to AgentOrchestratorAdapter interface."""
    for name in ADAPTER_REGISTRY:
        adapter = get_adapter(name, REPO_DIR)
        assert isinstance(adapter, AgentOrchestratorAdapter)
        assert hasattr(adapter, "run_workload")
        assert hasattr(adapter, "setup")
        assert hasattr(adapter, "cold_start_cmd")
        assert hasattr(adapter, "name")
        assert hasattr(adapter, "language")
        assert hasattr(adapter, "runtime")


def test_adapter_run_workload_across_targets(monkeypatch):
    """Verify each adapter runs workload W1 deterministically in standard test environment."""
    mock_port = 8088
    start_mock_server_if_needed(mock_port)
    monkeypatch.setenv("LLAMA_API_URL", f"http://127.0.0.1:{mock_port}/v1/chat/completions")

    for name in ADAPTER_REGISTRY:
        adapter = get_adapter(name, REPO_DIR)
        result = adapter.run_workload("W1")
        assert isinstance(result, dict)
        assert len(result) > 0


def test_fail_fast_on_unreachable_server(monkeypatch):
    """
    Verify fail-fast policy: when dry_run=False and the neural server port is offline,
    the pipeline immediately raises RuntimeError instead of generating synthetic data.
    """
    # Test health check against an offline loopback port (59999)
    offline_url = "http://127.0.0.1:59999/health"
    is_healthy = check_server_health(url=offline_url, timeout=0.2)
    assert is_healthy is False

    # Point LLAMA_HEALTH_URL to the offline port
    monkeypatch.setenv("LLAMA_HEALTH_URL", offline_url)

    # Verify run_campaign fails fast with RuntimeError when target port is offline
    with pytest.raises(RuntimeError) as exc_info:
        run_campaign(reps_per_condition=1, dry_run=False)

    assert "llama-server not reachable" in str(exc_info.value)

"""
Automated Cross-Artifact Numerical Consistency Verification Test
Teste de Verificação Automatizada de Consistência Numérica entre Artefatos

Author / Autor: Rafael Paniccia Cassetta (UFABC)
Language / Idioma: Bilingual (PT-BR & EN-US comments, English code)
"""

import csv
import json
import os

REPO_DIR = os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)))


def test_summary_statistics_matches_csv_table():
    """Verify metrics in summary_statistics.json match metrics_summary.csv exactly across all 20 cells."""
    json_path = os.path.join(REPO_DIR, "results", "publication_campaign", "summary_statistics.json")
    csv_path = os.path.join(REPO_DIR, "results", "publication_campaign", "tables", "metrics_summary.csv")

    assert os.path.exists(json_path), f"Missing {json_path}"
    assert os.path.exists(csv_path), f"Missing {csv_path}"

    with open(json_path, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        stats_data = json.load(f)

    with open(csv_path, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        reader = csv.DictReader(f)
        csv_rows = list(reader)

    # 5 agents x 4 workloads = 20 rows
    assert len(csv_rows) == 20, f"Expected 20 rows (5 agents x 4 workloads), got {len(csv_rows)}"

    for row in csv_rows:
        ak = row["agent"]
        w = row["workload"]
        assert ak in stats_data, f"Agent {ak} missing from summary JSON"
        assert w in stats_data[ak], f"Workload {w} missing from agent {ak} in summary JSON"
        expected = stats_data[ak][w]

        # Verify numerical consistency between CSV and JSON
        assert abs(float(row["t_task_median_ms"]) - expected["t_task_median_ms"]) < 1e-2
        assert abs(float(row["rss_peak_mb"]) - expected["rss_peak_mb"]) < 1e-2

    assert "W4 & PicoClaw (Go Native)" in content
    assert "W4 & OpenClaw (Node.js Full 54-Tool)" in content

def test_smc_power_monitor_slice_energy_synthetic():
    """Verify SMCPowerMonitor.slice_energy correctly calculates trapezoidal integration for synthetic samples."""
    from benchmark.telemetry import SMCPowerMonitor

    monitor = SMCPowerMonitor(sample_interval_ms=50)
    monitor.idle_package_mw = 1500.0
    monitor.active = True

    # 1. Test single sample fallback with elapsed_ms from timestamp interval
    t0 = 1000000000  # 1.0s in ns
    t1 = 2000000000  # 2.0s in ns (1000 ms elapsed)
    monitor._samples = [{"timestamp_ns": 1500000000, "elapsed_ms": 50.0, "package_mw": 5000.0, "cpu_mw": 3000.0, "gpu_mw": 1000.0, "ane_mw": 0.0}]

    res_single = monitor.slice_energy(t0, t1)
    assert res_single is not None
    # 5000 mW total for 1.0s = 5.0 J
    assert abs(res_single["energy_total_j"] - 5.0) < 0.1
    # 3500 mW net above 1500 mW idle for 1.0s = 3.5 J
    assert abs(res_single["energy_net_above_idle_j"] - 3.5) < 0.1

    # 2. Test multi-sample trapezoidal integration
    t_start = 0
    t_end = 300000000
    monitor._samples = [
        {"timestamp_ns": 50000000, "elapsed_ms": 50.0, "package_mw": 4000.0, "cpu_mw": 2000.0, "gpu_mw": 1000.0, "ane_mw": 0.0},
        {"timestamp_ns": 150000000, "elapsed_ms": 100.0, "package_mw": 6000.0, "cpu_mw": 3000.0, "gpu_mw": 2000.0, "ane_mw": 0.0},
        {"timestamp_ns": 250000000, "elapsed_ms": 100.0, "package_mw": 8000.0, "cpu_mw": 4000.0, "gpu_mw": 3000.0, "ane_mw": 0.0},
    ]
    res_multi = monitor.slice_energy(t_start, t_end)
    assert res_multi is not None
    assert res_multi["energy_total_j"] > 0.0
    assert res_multi["energy_net_above_idle_j"] > 0.0


def test_prompt_token_parity_across_equalized_arms(monkeypatch):
    """
    Verify that for workloads W1 and W2, prompt tokens and parity are strictly preserved
    across equalized arms (picoclaw, openclaw_minimal, python_native).
    """
    from benchmark.adapters import get_adapter
    from benchmark.run_unified_benchmark import start_mock_server_if_needed

    mock_port = 8092
    start_mock_server_if_needed(mock_port)
    monkeypatch.setenv("LLAMA_API_URL", f"http://127.0.0.1:{mock_port}/v1/chat/completions")

    w1_tokens = {}
    w2_tokens = {}

    for arm in ["picoclaw", "openclaw_minimal", "python_native"]:
        adapter = get_adapter(arm, REPO_DIR)
        res_w1 = adapter.run_workload("W1")
        res_w2 = adapter.run_workload("W2")

        assert res_w1["prompt_tokens"] > 0, f"{arm} W1 returned 0 prompt tokens"
        assert res_w2["prompt_tokens"] > 0, f"{arm} W2 returned 0 prompt tokens"
        w1_tokens[arm] = res_w1["prompt_tokens"]
        w2_tokens[arm] = res_w2["prompt_tokens"]

    # Verify parity under standardized mock environment
    assert w1_tokens["picoclaw"] == w1_tokens["openclaw_minimal"] == w1_tokens["python_native"]
    assert w2_tokens["picoclaw"] == w2_tokens["openclaw_minimal"] == w2_tokens["python_native"]

    # In publication campaign records, verify that every trial records prompt_tokens > 0
    campaign_file = os.path.join(REPO_DIR, "results", "publication_campaign", "raw_trials.jsonl")
    if os.path.exists(campaign_file):
        with open(campaign_file, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            for line in f:
                if not line.strip():
                    continue
                trial = json.loads(line)
                assert trial.get("prompt_tokens", 0) > 0, f"Zero prompt tokens recorded in trial: {trial}"






================================================================================
FILE: tests/test_statistical_engine.py
SHA-256: 357fb87f55273026224efc79a446fa2c25edaca8ddc133a7fc7544e6bb832e74
================================================================================
"""
Unit Tests for Statistical Inference Engine & Metrics
Testes Unitários para Motor de Inferência Estatística e Métricas

Author / Autor: Rafael Paniccia Cassetta (UFABC)
Language / Idioma: Bilingual (PT-BR & EN-US comments, English code)
"""

import numpy as np
from scipy import stats

from benchmark.analyze_results import (
    bootstrap_ci,
    cliffs_delta,
    holm_bonferroni_adjust,
    vargha_delaney_a12,
)


def compute_edp(energy_joules: float, latency_seconds: float) -> float:
    """Compute Energy-Delay Product (EDP) in Joule-seconds."""
    return energy_joules * latency_seconds


def test_edp_calculation():
    """Verify Energy-Delay Product calculation (Gonzalez & Horowitz, 1996)."""
    e = 15.0  # Joules
    t = 2.0   # seconds
    edp = compute_edp(e, t)
    assert edp == 30.0


def test_cliffs_delta_and_a12_extremes():
    """Verify Cliff's delta and Vargha-Delaney A12 for strictly dominated distributions."""
    sample_a = [10.0, 11.0, 12.0]
    sample_b = [20.0, 21.0, 22.0]

    # Every element in sample_a is less than every element in sample_b
    delta_ab = cliffs_delta(sample_a, sample_b)
    assert delta_ab == -1.0
    a12_ab = vargha_delaney_a12(sample_a, sample_b)
    assert a12_ab == 0.0

    # Inverted: sample_b vs sample_a
    delta_ba = cliffs_delta(sample_b, sample_a)
    assert delta_ba == 1.0
    a12_ba = vargha_delaney_a12(sample_b, sample_a)
    assert a12_ba == 1.0


def test_cliffs_delta_and_a12_identical():
    """Verify Cliff's delta and Vargha-Delaney A12 for identical distributions."""
    sample_a = [5.0, 5.0, 5.0]
    sample_b = [5.0, 5.0, 5.0]

    delta = cliffs_delta(sample_a, sample_b)
    assert delta == 0.0
    a12 = vargha_delaney_a12(sample_a, sample_b)
    assert a12 == 0.5


def test_bootstrap_ci_coverage():
    """Verify 95% bootstrap confidence interval contains the true population mean."""
    np.random.seed(42)
    # 50 samples from N(100, 5)
    data = np.random.normal(100.0, 5.0, size=50).tolist()
    low_ci, high_ci = bootstrap_ci(data, n_resamples=2000, alpha=0.05, seed=42)

    assert low_ci < 100.0 < high_ci


def test_holm_bonferroni_adjustment():
    """Verify family-wise error rate control via Holm-Bonferroni step-down adjustment."""
    # Raw p-values: smallest gets multiplied by m, second smallest by m-1, etc.
    # Also verify enforce monotonicity: adj_p[i] <= adj_p[i+1]
    raw_p = [0.005, 0.015, 0.040, 0.200]
    adj_p = holm_bonferroni_adjust(raw_p)

    assert len(adj_p) == len(raw_p)
    # Check that adjusted p-values are >= raw p-values and <= 1.0
    for r, a in zip(raw_p, adj_p):
        assert a >= r
        assert a <= 1.0

    # Check monotonicity
    for i in range(len(adj_p) - 1):
        assert adj_p[i] <= adj_p[i + 1]


def test_mann_whitney_significance():
    """Verify two-sided Mann-Whitney U test on separated samples."""
    sample_fast = [1.2, 1.3, 1.1, 1.4, 1.25]
    sample_slow = [5.2, 5.5, 5.3, 5.8, 5.4]

    u_stat, p_val = stats.mannwhitneyu(sample_fast, sample_slow, alternative="two-sided")
    assert p_val < 0.01
    d = cliffs_delta(sample_fast, sample_slow)
    assert d == -1.0

FILE: tests/test_telemetry_parser.py

"""
Unit Tests for Hardware Telemetry & Powermetrics Parsing
Testes Unitários para Telemetria de Hardware e Parser do Powermetrics

Author / Autor: Rafael Paniccia Cassetta (UFABC)
Language / Idioma: Bilingual (PT-BR & EN-US comments, English code)
"""

import sys

import pytest

from benchmark.telemetry import (
    LinuxRAPLMonitor,
    SMCPowerMonitor,
    get_process_rusage_v4,
    integrate_energy_trapezoidal,
    parse_powermetrics_sample,
)


def test_parse_sample_block():
    """Verify powermetrics sample parsing into CPU, GPU, ANE and package power."""
    sample_raw = """
    *** Sampled system activity (Mon Nov 10 20:00:00 2026 -0300) (100.12ms elapsed) ***

    CPU Power: 1250 mW
    GPU Power: 11400 mW
    ANE Power: 0 mW
    Combined Power (CPU + GPU + ANE): 12650 mW
    """
    parsed = parse_powermetrics_sample(sample_raw)
    assert parsed["cpu_mw"] == 1250.0
    assert parsed["gpu_mw"] == 11400.0
    assert parsed["ane_mw"] == 0.0
    assert parsed["package_mw"] == 12650.0


def test_parse_sample_fallback_combined():
    """Verify fallback summation if Combined Power header is absent."""
    sample_raw = """
    CPU Power: 2000 mW
    GPU Power: 3000 mW
    """
    parsed = parse_powermetrics_sample(sample_raw)
    assert parsed["cpu_mw"] == 2000.0
    assert parsed["gpu_mw"] == 3000.0
    assert parsed["package_mw"] == 5000.0


def test_integrate_energy_trapezoidal():
    """Verify trapezoidal numerical integration of power samples over monotonic timestamps."""
    # 0.0s: 10 W (10000 mW)
    # 1.0s: 20 W (20000 mW) -> trapezoid area = 1.0 * (10 + 20) / 2 = 15 J
    # 2.0s: 20 W (20000 mW) -> rectangle area = 1.0 * (20 + 20) / 2 = 20 J
    # Total expected: 35.0 J
    samples = [
        {"timestamp_ns": 0, "package_mw": 10000.0, "cpu_mw": 6000.0, "gpu_mw": 4000.0, "ane_mw": 0.0},
        {"timestamp_ns": 1_000_000_000, "package_mw": 20000.0, "cpu_mw": 12000.0, "gpu_mw": 8000.0, "ane_mw": 0.0},
        {"timestamp_ns": 2_000_000_000, "package_mw": 20000.0, "cpu_mw": 12000.0, "gpu_mw": 8000.0, "ane_mw": 0.0},
    ]
    summary = integrate_energy_trapezoidal(samples)
    assert pytest.approx(summary["energy_total_j"], 0.001) == 35.0
    assert summary["duration_s"] == 2.0


def test_integrate_energy_empty_or_single():
    """Verify empty sample list yields 0.0 Joules."""
    res_empty = integrate_energy_trapezoidal([])
    assert res_empty["energy_total_j"] == 0.0

    res_single = integrate_energy_trapezoidal([{
        "timestamp_ns": 0,
        "elapsed_ms": 100.0,
        "package_mw": 5000.0,
        "cpu_mw": 2000.0,
        "gpu_mw": 3000.0,
        "ane_mw": 0.0
    }])
    assert res_single["energy_total_j"] > 0.0


def test_get_process_rusage_v4():
    """Verify Darwin libproc hardware counters on macOS, or None fallback on Linux."""
    res = get_process_rusage_v4()
    if sys.platform == "darwin":
        assert res is not None
        assert "proc_instructions" in res
        assert "proc_cycles" in res
        assert "proc_rss_mb" in res
        assert isinstance(res["proc_instructions"], int)
        assert isinstance(res["proc_cycles"], int)
    else:
        # On Linux without libproc, either returns psutil dict or None
        assert res is None or "proc_rss_mb" in res


def test_smc_monitor_no_sudo_degradation():
    """Verify SMCPowerMonitor non-root graceful degradation without crashing."""
    monitor = SMCPowerMonitor()
    monitor.start()
    res = monitor.stop()
    assert res is None or isinstance(res, dict)


def test_rapl_monitor_graceful_handling():
    """Verify LinuxRAPLMonitor graceful fallback when sysfs nodes are unavailable."""
    monitor = LinuxRAPLMonitor()
    monitor.start()
    res = monitor.stop()
    assert res is None or isinstance(res, dict)

"""
Unit Tests for Deterministic Workload Datasets
Testes Unitários para Datasets Determinísticos dos Workloads

Author / Autor: Rafael Paniccia Cassetta (UFABC)
"""

import json
import os

import numpy as np

REPO_DIR = os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)))
DATA_DIR = os.path.join(REPO_DIR, "data", "workload_data")


def test_mock_files_directory_structure():
    """Verify mock_files contains exactly 50 synthetic deterministic files."""
    mock_dir = os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "mock_files")
    assert os.path.exists(mock_dir), f"Directory {mock_dir} does not exist"
    files = [f for f in os.listdir(mock_dir) if not f.startswith(".")]
    assert len(files) == 50, f"Expected 50 mock files, found {len(files)}"
    for f in files:
        full_p = os.path.join(mock_dir, f)
        assert os.path.getsize(full_p) > 0, f"File {f} is empty"


def test_system_logs_schema_and_record_count():
    """Verify system_logs.jsonl contains 750 valid JSON lines with required fields."""
    log_file = os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "system_logs.jsonl")
    assert os.path.exists(log_file), f"Log file {log_file} does not exist"

    count = 0
    with open(log_file, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        for idx, line in enumerate(f):
            count += 1
            record = json.loads(line)
            assert "timestamp" in record, f"Line {idx} missing timestamp"
            assert "subsystem" in record, f"Line {idx} missing subsystem"
            assert "level" in record, f"Line {idx} missing level"
            assert "latency_ms" in record, f"Line {idx} missing latency_ms"
            assert isinstance(record["latency_ms"], (int, float)), f"Line {idx} latency_ms not numeric"

    assert count == 750, f"Expected 750 log entries, counted {count}"


def test_rag_chunks_count_and_format():
    """Verify rag_chunks_10k.json contains valid text chunks with index keys."""
    rag_file = os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "rag_chunks_10k.json")
    assert os.path.exists(rag_file), f"RAG chunks file {rag_file} does not exist"

    with open(rag_file, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        chunks = json.load(f)

    assert len(chunks) >= 100, f"Expected at least 100 chunks, found {len(chunks)}"
    assert "0" in chunks, "Index 0 missing in chunks"
    assert isinstance(chunks["0"], str) and len(chunks["0"]) > 50


def test_embedding_head_matrix_dimensions():
    """Verify embedding_head_100k.npz contains the dense projection matrix."""
    emb_file = os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "embedding_head_100k.npz")
    assert os.path.exists(emb_file), f"Embedding weights {emb_file} missing"

    data = np.load(emb_file)
    assert len(data.files) > 0
    key = data.files[0]
    mat = data[key]
    assert mat.dtype in (np.float32, np.float64)
    assert mat.size >= 98000, f"Matrix size {mat.size} below expected ~98k parameters"

\begin{frontmatter}

\title{A Reproducible Benchmark Suite for Isolating Orchestration Overhead and Schema Prefill Costs in Local LLM Agents on Apple Silicon}

\author[ufabc]{Rafael Paniccia Cassetta\corref{cor1}}
\ead{rafael.paniccia@aluno.ufabc.edu.br}
\cortext[cor1]{Corresponding author.}
\affiliation[ufabc]{organization={Centro de Engenharia, Modelagem e Ci\^encias Sociais Aplicadas, Universidade Federal do ABC (UFABC)},
            city={Santo Andr\'e},
            state={S\~ao Paulo},
            country={Brazil}}

\begin{abstract}
As autonomous AI agents shift from remote cloud endpoints to local edge deployment, software execution runtime overhead becomes a critical bottleneck competing with neural weights for unified memory and compute bandwidth. We introduce the \texttt{picoclaw-vs-openclaw-benchmark} suite, an extensible, journal-grade benchmarking framework designed to isolate orchestrator delay ($T_{\text{orch}}$) from neural inference latency ($T_{\text{neural}}$) on Apple Silicon SoC microarchitectures. The framework implements the Strategy pattern to evaluate native compiled binaries (Go Mach-O ARM64) against JIT-compiled runtimes (TypeScript/Node.js V8) and interpreted baselines (Python 3.12) across four standardized workload tiers (W1--W4), featuring unconstrained feature ablation (54 tools vs. minimal equalized tools). Experimental telemetry combines non-intrusive Darwin XNU \texttt{libproc} performance counters, Apple System Management Controller (SMC) electrical current sampling, and robust non-parametric statistical inference (median and interquartile range, two-sided Mann-Whitney $U$ test with Holm-Bonferroni correction, Cliff's Delta $d$, and Vargha-Delaney $\hat{A}_{12}$ with 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals). Furthermore, the software provides a reviewer-friendly portable simulation mode (\texttt{make dry-run}) executing in under ten seconds across macOS and Linux without superuser privileges or local weights.
\end{abstract}

\begin{keyword}
AI Agent Orchestration \sep Benchmarking Framework \sep Apple Silicon M4 \sep Energy-Delay Product \sep Non-Parametric Statistics \sep Software Reproducibility
\end{keyword}

\end{frontmatter}

\section{Motivation and Significance}
\label{sec:motivation}
Autonomous agentic workflows employing the ReAct (Reasoning and Acting) paradigm \cite{yao2023react} require orchestrators to iteratively parse tool schemas, validate arguments, dispatch operating system operations, and format prompts for Large Language Model (LLM) inference \cite{schick2023toolformer}. On unified memory microarchitectures such as Apple Silicon (UMA @ 120 GB/s), agent orchestrators share physical LPDDR5X DRAM channels with GPU compute shaders running quantized neural inference (\texttt{llama.cpp}) \cite{reddi2020mlperf}. 

Prior benchmarks predominantly evaluate LLM reasoning accuracy on cloud APIs \cite{reddi2020mlperf,llmperf2023,liu2023agentbench}, obscuring client-side runtime bottlenecks and context reuse dynamics \cite{kwon2023vllm,zheng2024sglang,patel2024splitwise}. Software runtime overhead (e.g., V8 garbage collection, context serialization, dynamic schema validation) directly impairs execution latency and increases the Energy-Delay Product ($\text{EDP} = E \times T$) \cite{horowitz1994low,gonzalez1996energy,barroso2007case,pereira2021ranking,georgiou2022energy,you2023zeus}. 

To bridge this gap, we developed \texttt{picoclaw-vs-openclaw-benchmark}, an open-source, extensible framework providing:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Rigorous mathematical isolation of orchestrator delay: $T_{\text{task}} = T_{\text{neural}} + T_{\text{orch}}$, with microsecond precision;
    \item Physical hardware telemetry leveraging Apple SMC shunt sampling alongside Darwin XNU \texttt{libproc} instruction counters and Linux RAPL energy interfaces;
    \item Controlled ablation quantifying the cost of 54 enterprise tools versus an equalized minimal configuration;
    \item Reference cross-language comparison encompassing Go (compiled native), Node.js (JIT V8), and Python 3.12 (interpreted reference);
    \item Multi-platform reviewer reproducibility with zero external dependencies and graceful unprivileged non-root fallback.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Notice of Scope and Trademarks}
This benchmarking suite evaluates clean-room, isomorphic reference implementations in Go (\texttt{picoclaw\_orch.go}), Node.js/TypeScript (\texttt{openclaw\_orch.mjs}), and Python (\texttt{python\_orch.py}) designed to evaluate the systems-level trade-offs of compiled native versus JIT-compiled/interpreted agent architectures under identical prompts and tool schemas. PicoClaw and OpenClaw are trademarks of their respective creators; this software is an independent academic performance benchmark and is not affiliated with or endorsed by upstream project maintainers.

\section{Software Description}
\label{sec:software_description}

\subsection{Comparison with Related Benchmarking Suites}
Prior benchmarking tools primarily address deep learning model inference throughput, distributed cloud LLM serving, or GPU training power, leaving client-side cross-language runtime isolation and Apple Silicon SMC energy attribution unaddressed. Table~\ref{tab:related_tools} compares our framework with representative performance evaluation tools.

\begin{table}[h!]
\small
\centering
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Benchmark / Suite} & \textbf{Primary Domain} & \textbf{Orchestrator Isolation ($T_{\text{orch}}$)} & \textbf{Physical Telemetry} & \textbf{Runtimes} \\
\midrule
MLPerf Inference \cite{reddi2020mlperf} & Neural model throughput & No ($T_{\text{model}}$ only) & External meter only & C++ / Python \\
LLMPerf \cite{llmperf2023} & Cloud LLM serving/TTFT & No (Network HTTP only) & No (Cloud API) & Python only \\
Zeus \cite{you2023zeus} & GPU energy (DNN training) & No & NVML / Linux RAPL & Python / PyTorch \\
AgentBench \cite{liu2023agentbench} & Agent reasoning accuracy & No (Aggregate task time) & No & Python only \\
\textbf{This Work} & \textbf{Agent runtime overhead} & \textbf{Yes ($T_{\text{orch}} = T_{\text{task}} - T_{\text{neural}}$)} & \textbf{Apple SMC + Linux RAPL} & \textbf{Go, Node.js V8, Python} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Comparative analysis of \texttt{picoclaw-vs-openclaw-benchmark} against related systems benchmarking tools.}
\label{tab:related_tools}
\end{table}

\subsection{Software Architecture}
The framework is designed around an extensible Object-Oriented Adapter pattern (\texttt{benchmark/adapters/}), decoupling experimental workload generation from specific orchestrator runtime implementations:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Adapter Layer (\texttt{AgentOrchestratorAdapter}):} Abstract strategy defining standardized lifecycle hooks: \texttt{setup()}, \texttt{cold\_start\_cmd()} (benchmarked via statistical sampling \cite{hyperfine2024}), \texttt{run\_workload(w\_id)}, and \texttt{teardown()}. Registered implementations include:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \texttt{PicoClawAdapter}: Compiled native Mach-O ARM64 binary written in Go 1.23;
        \item \texttt{OpenClawAdapter}: Node.js / V8 runtime evaluating both \texttt{minimal} (15 tools) and \texttt{full} (54 tools) schema loads;
        \item \texttt{PythonNativeAdapter}: Interpreted baseline in pure Python 3.12 with NumPy vector projections;
        \item \texttt{NeuralBaselineAdapter}: Direct HTTP streaming pass-through measuring zero-overhead neural latency.
    \end{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Deterministic Workload Generator (\texttt{data/workload\_data/}):} Provides four difficulty tiers:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \textbf{W1 (Simple Reflection):} In-memory synthesis (64 tokens);
        \item \textbf{W2 (3-Step Tool Chain):} APFS filesystem inspection, regex parsing, and numerical statistics (128 tokens);
        \item \textbf{W3 (Vector RAG):} Cosine similarity ranking over 10,000 document chunks via a deterministic projection head (256 tokens);
        \item \textbf{W4 (10-Turn ReAct):} Multi-step autonomous decision loop executing 10 tool invocations (640 tokens).
    \end{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Hardware and Kernel Telemetry Engine:} Combines Apple SMC \texttt{powermetrics} (50 ms milliwatt current sampling) with unprivileged Darwin kernel \texttt{proc\_pid\_rusage} (recording resident set size, retired CPU instructions, and core cycles).
\end{itemize}

The modular architecture topology and non-intrusive telemetry pipeline are depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:arch_topology}.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../figures/fig1_architecture_topology.png}
\caption{System architecture and telemetry topology of the \texttt{picoclaw-vs-openclaw-benchmark} suite. The benchmark runner orchestrates standardized workloads across isolated agent adapters communicating over localhost loopback TCP with the Metal GPU neural daemon, while concurrent daemons capture Darwin kernel \texttt{libproc} counters and Apple SMC power shunts.}
\label{fig:arch_topology}
\end{figure}

The framework's extensible design is demonstrated in Listing~\ref{lst:rust_adapter}, showing how a new native agent orchestrator (e.g., \texttt{RustRigAdapter} targeting a high-performance compiled Rust binary) can be integrated in under 35 lines of Python code:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python, caption={Extending the benchmark suite with a native Rust agent orchestrator adapter (\texttt{rust\_rig\_adapter.py}).}, label={lst:rust_adapter}]
from benchmark.adapters.base import AgentOrchestratorAdapter
from benchmark.telemetry import run_subprocess_with_rusage
import os, json

class RustRigAdapter(AgentOrchestratorAdapter):
    """Native Rust agent orchestrator adapter implementing the lifecycle contract."""
    def __init__(self, repo_dir: str):
        super().__init__(
            name="RustRig",
            runtime="Rust 1.80 (Native Mach-O ARM64)",
            language="Rust",
            mode="native_release"
        )
        self.bin_path = os.path.join(repo_dir, "bin", "rust_rig_agent")

    def setup(self) -> bool:
        return os.path.exists(self.bin_path)

    def cold_start_cmd(self) -> list[str]:
        return [self.bin_path, "--version"]

    def run_workload(self, workload_id: str, prompt: str = "", iterations: int = 1) -> dict:
        cmd = [self.bin_path, "--workload", workload_id, "--reps", str(iterations)]
        stdout, stderr, rc, hw_stats = run_subprocess_with_rusage(cmd)
        if rc != 0:
            raise RuntimeError(f"RustRig execution failed: {stderr}")
        res = json.loads(stdout.strip())
        return {**res[0], **hw_stats}
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Software Functionalities}
The analysis engine (\texttt{benchmark/analyze\_results.py}) computes formal computer systems metrics and publication-ready LaTeX tables:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Energy-Delay Product (EDP):} Computes total energy $E = \int P(t) dt$ and active energy above quiescent baseline, yielding $\text{EDP} = E \times T_{\text{task}}$ ($\text{J}\cdot\text{s}$) \cite{gonzalez1996energy}, alongside Joules per generated token.
    \item \textbf{Non-Parametric Central Tendency and Dispersion:} Reports median and Interquartile Range ($\text{IQR} = Q_3 - Q_1$) to prevent outlier distortion from GPU prefill and garbage collection events, supplemented by empirical mean $\pm$ standard deviation and 95th percentile ($p_{95}$) tail latency.
    \item \textbf{Statistical Hypothesis Testing and Effect Sizes:} Conducts two-sided Mann-Whitney $U$ tests \cite{mann1947test} adjusted for multiple comparisons via the Holm-Bonferroni step-down procedure \cite{holm1979simple}, Cliff's Delta $d \in [-1, 1]$ \cite{cliff1993dominance}, and Vargha-Delaney $\hat{A}_{12} \in [0, 1]$ \cite{vargha2000critique} with 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals (2,000 resamples), establishing stochastic dominance without assuming Gaussian distributions.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Hardware Constraints, Permissions, and Third-Party Licenses}
In unified-memory System-on-Chip (SoC) architectures such as Apple Silicon M4, physical electrical shunt sensors managed by the System Management Controller (SMC) instrument aggregate hardware power rails (CPU clusters, Metal GPU, and ANE) at the package level rather than individual operating system processes \cite{barroso2007case,you2023zeus}. Consequently, isolating exclusive analog Joules for a single POSIX process during concurrent CPU--GPU execution is physically unobservable at the hardware shunt layer. Following established energy-benchmarking methodology for machine learning systems \cite{you2023zeus,georgiou2022energy}, our harness resolves this architectural constraint via two-layer telemetry: (i) system-wide package energy ($E_{\text{total}}$ and net active energy above quiescent idle baseline) integrated via the composite trapezoidal rule from \texttt{powermetrics} at 50~ms intervals, and (ii) per-process microarchitectural attribution (\texttt{ri\_instructions}, \texttt{ri\_cycles}, \texttt{ri\_billed\_energy}, and \texttt{ri\_lifetime\_max\_phys\_footprint}) polled from the Darwin XNU kernel via \texttt{libproc} (\texttt{proc\_pid\_rusage} with \texttt{RUSAGE\_INFO\_V4}).

Direct access to Apple SMC hardware sensors via \texttt{powermetrics} requires elevated \texttt{root} privileges due to Darwin XNU kernel protections against microarchitectural power side-channel vulnerabilities. Crucially, the benchmark runner enforces privilege isolation: all evaluated agent runtimes, orchestrator subprocesses, and the \texttt{llama-server} neural daemon execute strictly in unprivileged user space. When administrative privileges are absent, the telemetry layer degrades gracefully without synthetic estimation, recording latency, memory footprint, and \texttt{libproc} counters while marking electrical energy fields as unavailable (\texttt{N/A}).

All benchmarked software components are governed by permissive open-source licenses: \texttt{llama.cpp} is licensed under the MIT License \cite{llamacpp2024}; \texttt{PicoClaw} reference implementation is distributed under the MIT License \cite{picoclaw2026}; \texttt{OpenClaw} reference implementation is provided under the MIT License \cite{openclaw2026}; the neural model weights (Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct) are governed by the Meta Llama 3.2 Community License; and the benchmark harness itself is released under the MIT License, ensuring unconstrained academic reproducibility and extensibility.

\section{Illustrative Examples and Empirical Results}
\label{sec:examples}

\input{../results/publication_campaign/tables/macros_empirical.tex}

\subsection{Consolidated Experimental Matrix}
Table~\ref{tab:evaluation_ablation_m4} is dynamically generated from \texttt{raw\_trials.jsonl} via the statistical pipeline (\texttt{benchmark/analyze\_results.py}), reflecting all five evaluated configurations across workloads W1--W4. The empirical distributions of end-to-end task latency ($T_{\text{task}}$) and isolated orchestrator delay ($T_{\text{orch}}$) are illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:latency_overhead}.

\input{../results/publication_campaign/tables/table_journal_ablation.tex}

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../figures/fig3_exp2_latency_overhead.png}
\caption{Empirical task latency ($T_{\text{task}}$) and isolated orchestrator delay ($T_{\text{orch}}$) across workloads W1--W4 on Apple Silicon M4. Compiled Go execution exhibits lower serialization and dispatch delay ($T_{\text{orch}}$) while end-to-end task duration remains bounded by neural inference latency under equalized token generation.}
\label{fig:latency_overhead}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Analytical Finding: Cold Prefill Stall in W3 and Dispersion Dynamics}
Analysis of the 600 evaluated trial records ($n=30$ per condition) in \texttt{raw\_trials.jsonl} demonstrates the necessity of non-parametric statistics on unified memory architectures \cite{jain1991art,lilja2000measuring}. In vector similarity ranking (W3), PicoClaw exhibits an empirical median task latency of $\WThreePicoMedian\text{ ms}$ with an Interquartile Range ($\text{IQR}$) of $\WThreePicoIQR\text{ ms}$ (mean $\WThreePicoMean \pm \WThreePicoSD\text{ ms}$), compared to $\WThreeOpenMinMedian\text{ ms}$ ($\text{IQR} = \WThreeOpenMinIQR\text{ ms}$) for OpenClaw Minimal and $\WThreeOpenFullMedian\text{ ms}$ ($\text{IQR} = \WThreeOpenFullIQR\text{ ms}$) for OpenClaw Full. Stochastic dominance testing confirms PicoClaw significantly outperforms OpenClaw Minimal (Cliff's Delta $d = \WThreeCliffsDeltaMin$, $\hat{A}_{12} = \WThreeAOneTwoMin$, Holm-Bonferroni adjusted $p \WThreeAdjPMin$) and OpenClaw Full ($d = \WThreeCliffsDeltaFull$, $\hat{A}_{12} = \WThreeAOneTwoFull$, adjusted $p \WThreeAdjPFull$). Isolated orchestrator delay in W3 confirms Go runtime efficiency: PicoClaw incurs a median $T_{\text{orch}}$ of only $\WThreePicoOrchMedian\text{ ms}$ vs. $\WThreeOpenMinOrchMedian\text{ ms}$ for OpenClaw Minimal and $\WThreeOpenFullOrchMedian\text{ ms}$ for OpenClaw Full.

\subsection{Orchestration Overhead Isolation and Cross-Language Tool-Chain Dynamics in W2}
In the three-step tool chain workload (W2), aggregate physical task metrics demonstrate end-to-end latency parity between native and managed orchestrators: PicoClaw recorded a median task latency of $\WTwoPicoMedian\text{ ms}$ (mean $\WTwoPicoMean\text{ ms}$, $\WTwoPicoEnergy\text{ J}$) compared to $\WTwoOpenMinMedian\text{ ms}$ (mean $\WTwoOpenMinMean\text{ ms}$, $\WTwoOpenMinEnergy\text{ J}$) for OpenClaw Minimal, closely matching the direct NeuralBaseline ($\WTwoNeuralMedian\text{ ms}$, mean $\WTwoNeuralMean\text{ ms}$, $\WTwoNeuralEnergy\text{ J}$). Granular inspection of \texttt{raw\_trials.jsonl} confirms that under greedy autoregressive decoding with 15 standardized schema definitions, the underlying LLaMA-3.2-3B model emitted an identical token count of $\WTwoPicoTokens$ tokens for PicoClaw ($T_{\text{neural}} = \WTwoNeuralPico\text{ ms}$) and $\WTwoOpenMinTokens$ tokens for OpenClaw Minimal ($T_{\text{neural}} = \WTwoNeuralOpenMin\text{ ms}$), achieving complete end-to-end parity within $1\text{ ms}$ ($5556.1\text{ ms}$ vs. $5557.1\text{ ms}$).

Crucially, when isolating runtime orchestrator overhead ($T_{\text{orch}}$) and separating client-side dispatch delay ($T_{\text{dispatch}}$) from HTTP transport latency ($T_{\text{http}}$), the architectural characteristics of the three language runtimes become clearly measurable:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Go Native (\texttt{PicoClaw}):} Achieves the lowest median orchestrator delay of $T_{\text{orch}} = \WTwoPicoOrchMedian\text{ ms}$ (mean $\WTwoPicoOrchMean\text{ ms}$), combining $T_{\text{dispatch}} = 1.78\text{ ms}$ with $T_{\text{http}} \approx 3.80\text{ ms}$ powered by Go's native \texttt{net/http} stack and non-blocking goroutines.
    \item \textbf{CPython Reference (\texttt{PythonNative}):} Incurs an intermediate median overhead of $T_{\text{orch}} = 11.23\text{ ms}$ ($T_{\text{dispatch}} = 1.00\text{ ms}$, $T_{\text{http}} \approx 10.23\text{ ms}$), reflecting synchronous socket I/O in Python's standard \texttt{urllib.request}.
    \item \textbf{Node.js V8 (\texttt{OpenClaw Minimal}):} Registers a median $T_{\text{orch}} = \WTwoOpenMinOrchMedian\text{ ms}$ (mean $\WTwoOpenMinOrchMean\text{ ms}$), where dispatch requires $1.31\text{ ms}$ while the V8 asynchronous event loop and Undici/\texttt{fetch} stream pipeline add $19.64\text{ ms}$ of socket buffer translation and GC book-keeping.
\end{itemize}
Overall, Go achieves $\WTwoOrchSpeedup\times$ lower orchestrator latency ($T_{\text{orch}}$) than Node.js Minimal. Furthermore, in physical DRAM footprint measured via Darwin kernel \texttt{libproc}, PicoClaw required a peak resident footprint of merely $\WTwoPicoRSS\text{ MB}$ versus $38.22\text{ MB}$ for Python and $\WTwoOpenMinRSS\text{ MB}$ for Node.js V8 ($\WTwoRAMReduction\times$ lower DRAM allocation). Token-normalized physical energy consumption confirms near-identical efficiency ($\WTwoPicoJoulePerToken\text{ J/token}$ for PicoClaw vs. $\WTwoOpenMinJoulePerToken\text{ J/token}$ for OpenClaw Minimal), demonstrating that client-side orchestrator choice dictates host resource utilization ($\text{CPU cycles}$, $\text{instructions}$, and $\text{RAM}$) rather than GPU energy saturation during prompt prefill. To guarantee full scientific provenance and traceability across revisions, the pre-fix raw observations are preserved in \texttt{results/exploratory\_campaigns/raw\_trials\_pre\_w2\_fix.jsonl}, while the evaluated dataset reflects client-side SSE parser standardization and tool descriptor token equalization.

\subsection{Controlled Tool Catalog Schema Prefill and KV-Cache Reuse Characterization (\texttt{llama.cpp} / Metal GPU)}
To address the critical distinction between orchestrator-induced dispatch delay ($T_{\text{orch}}$) and prompt-prefill attention computation ($T_{\text{prefill}}$) on unified memory architectures \cite{kwon2023vllm,gim2024promptcache,zheng2024sglang,patel2024splitwise,reddi2020mlperf,llmperf2023}, we conducted a controlled schema ablation directly against the local inference engine (\texttt{llama.cpp}) across 8 tool catalog sizes $T \in \{0, 5, 10, 15, 25, 40, 54, 100\}$ and prompt-cache configurations ($\texttt{cache\_prompt} \in \{\text{false}, \text{true}\}$, $n=30$ independent physical evaluations per cell following 3 priming warmups). This controlled experiment explicitly characterizes the prefill scaling behavior of the underlying Metal GPU inference server under progressive tool schema injection ($0$ to $100$ schemas), providing an empirical baseline for the impact of prompt bloat independently of host orchestrator execution. The consolidated empirical results are presented in Table~\ref{tab:prefill_ablation}.

\input{../results/publication_campaign/tables/table_prefill_ablation.tex}

As documented in Table~\ref{tab:prefill_ablation}, in-memory schema serialization and dispatch delay ($T_{\text{dispatch}}$) remains negligible ($< 2\text{ ms}$). However, un-cached prompt prefill latency ($T_{\text{prefill}}$) on the Metal GPU scales steeply with catalog dimension: prefill latency increases from approximately $128\text{ ms}$ at $0$ tools ($60$ tokens) to over $4{,}400\text{ ms}$ at $15$ tools ($2{,}081$ tokens), $17{,}900\text{ ms}$ at $54$ tools ($7{,}190$ tokens), and reaching nearly $39{,}000\text{ ms}$ at $100$ tools ($13{,}216$ prompt tokens), dominating total execution duration.

Regression analysis over the empirical trials in \texttt{prefill\_ablation.jsonl} confirms super-linear scaling: a power-law fit ($T_{\text{prefill}} = 0.3197 \cdot N^{1.2342}$ with 95\% CI $\alpha \in [1.2324, 1.2360]$, $R^2 = 0.9993$, $\text{AIC}_c = 99.25$) and quadratic model ($T_{\text{prefill}} = 7.895 \times 10^{-5} N^2 + 1.9201 N + 17.36\text{ ms}$, $R^2 = 0.99999$, $\text{AIC}_c = 68.88$) decisively outperform linear prefill approximations ($R^2 = 0.9889$, $\text{AIC}_c = 120.84$), matching the computational complexity of transformer self-attention over extended context windows before cache allocation. Crucially, activating KV-cache reuse ($\texttt{cache\_prompt} = \text{true}$) bypasses redundant attention evaluations across identical schema prefixes, unlocking a massive $5.5\times$ to $946.5\times$ speedup on prefill latency (reducing TTFT to sub-second levels across all tool catalog dimensions). This empirical characterization confirms that stock enterprise multi-tool agent schemas impair execution primarily through super-linear neural attention prefill scaling rather than host orchestrator delay \cite{kwon2023vllm,gim2024promptcache,zheng2024sglang,patel2024splitwise,schick2023toolformer}.

\subsection{Comparative Analysis: Cold Prefill Regime vs. Realistic KV-Cache Reuse Regime}
To evaluate runtime performance under production deployment scenarios where common tool catalogs and system prompts persist across agent turns, we conducted a companion campaign of 600 evaluated physical trials (60 priming warmups excluded) under active KV-cache prefix retention (\texttt{cache\_prompt=true}, $n=30$ independent physical evaluations per condition, block-isolated to eliminate single-slot cache thrashing in \texttt{llama-server}). The empirical results are presented in Table~\ref{tab:evaluation_warm_cache_m4}.

\input{../results/publication_campaign/tables/table_warm_cache.tex}

Contrasting the cold prefill regime (Regime A, Table~\ref{tab:evaluation_ablation_m4}) and the warm cache regime (Regime B, Table~\ref{tab:evaluation_warm_cache_m4}) reveals the operational trade-offs of edge agent execution:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Regime A (\texttt{cache\_prompt=false}):} Cold prompt attention prefill on the Metal GPU accounts for $>75\%$ of end-to-end task latency across all configurations. Because the GPU saturates package power at $\approx 16\text{ W}$ during attention computation, total latency and active energy remain dominated by neural matrix evaluation, producing near-parity in wall-clock duration between host runtimes.
    \item \textbf{Regime B (\texttt{cache\_prompt=true}):} When prompt prefill is amortized through verified block-isolated KV-cache prefix retention in \texttt{llama-server} ($\text{Cache Hit}$ of $99.9\%$ to $100.0\%$ in W1--W3 and $97.6\%$ to $99.3\%$ in W4; 20 randomized condition blocks with 3 priming warmups and 30 consecutive evaluations per block in single-slot \texttt{-np 1}), overall task duration drops dramatically: in W2, task latency falls to sub-second levels ($725.9\text{ ms}$ for PicoClaw, $752.3\text{ ms}$ for OpenClaw Minimal, and $913.5\text{ ms}$ for OpenClaw Full---a $21.6\times$ reduction for the 54-tool catalog compared to un-cached cold execution). Across W1--W4, Go Native (PicoClaw), PythonNative, and Node.js Minimal remain within $\pm 1.7\%$ to $4.2\%$ of the direct Neural Baseline ($722.3\text{ ms}$ in W2). Crucially, under this amortized prefill regime, host orchestrator overhead is no longer masked by GPU attention stalls, elevating the relative orchestration share ($\Phi_{\text{orch}} = T_{\text{orch}} / T_{\text{task}}$) to clearly measurable intervals between $0.37\%$ and $4.65\%$ ($T_{\text{dispatch}} = 1.90\text{ ms}$ and $T_{\text{http}} = 3.72\text{ ms}$ for Go; $T_{\text{dispatch}} = 1.02\text{ ms}$ and $T_{\text{http}} = 9.89\text{ ms}$ for Python; $T_{\text{dispatch}} = 1.28\text{ ms}$ and $T_{\text{http}} = 19.58\text{ ms}$ for Node.js Minimal in W2), demonstrating that language runtime efficiency becomes discriminative when inference stalls are eliminated.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Non-Parametric Spearman Rank Correlation Matrix across Telemetry Dimensions}
To systematically evaluate the coupling between execution dimensions, Table~\ref{tab:spearman_correlation} reports the pairwise Spearman rank correlation matrix ($\rho$) across 12 key systems telemetry dimensions for both regimes ($1{,}200$ total physical evaluations).

\input{../results/publication_campaign/tables/table_spearman_correlation.tex}

In Regime A (Cold Prefill), prompt attention computation strictly dominates global latency ($T_{\text{task}}$) and active net energy ($E_{\text{net}}$), yielding near-perfect rank alignment with prompt token length ($\rho = +0.948$, $p < 10^{-5}$ for $T_{\text{task}}$ vs. $N_{\text{prompt}}$; $\rho = +0.930$, $p < 10^{-5}$ for $T_{\text{task}}$ vs. $E_{\text{net}}$). In contrast, in Regime B (Warm KV-Cache Reuse), KV-cache amortization significantly decouples task latency from prompt tokens ($\rho = +0.586$, $p < 10^{-5}$), unmasking runtime execution dynamics: retired CPU instructions strongly govern orchestrator delay ($\rho = +0.824$, $p < 10^{-5}$), while client-side dispatch latency accounts for high rank agreement ($\rho = +0.845$, $p < 10^{-5}$). Furthermore, orchestrator overhead share ($\Phi_{\text{orch}} = T_{\text{orch}} / T_{\text{task}}$) spans $0.37\%$ to $4.65\%$ across workloads under warm cache, with measurement noise bounded within sub-millisecond precision via unprivileged Darwin kernel \texttt{libproc} telemetry.

\subsection{Key Findings, Hardware Microarchitecture, and Effect Sizes}
Across all 600 physical trials on the Apple Silicon M4 testbed, three core empirical conclusions emerge:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Microarchitectural Efficiency:} Hardware performance counters captured via Darwin XNU kernel \texttt{libproc} reveal that PicoClaw executes an average of $\PicoInstructionsMeanM \times 10^6$ retired CPU instructions and $\PicoCyclesMeanM \times 10^6$ CPU cycles, compared to $\OpenMinInstructionsMeanM \times 10^6$ instructions ($\InstructionOverheadRatio\times$ overhead) and $\OpenMinCyclesMeanM \times 10^6$ cycles ($\CyclesOverheadRatio\times$ overhead) for OpenClaw Minimal.
    \item \textbf{Physical DRAM Footprint Reduction:} PicoClaw maintains a peak resident set size (RSS) between $\PicoRSSMin\text{ MB}$ (W1) and $\PicoRSSWFour\text{ MB}$ (W4) (rising to $\PicoRSSWThree\text{ MB}$ during W3 10,000-vector cosine dot products), averaging $\PicoRSSOverallMean\text{ MB}$ overall. In contrast, OpenClaw demands $\OpenRSSMin\text{ MB}$ to $\OpenRSSMax\text{ MB}$ (averaging $\OpenRSSOverallMean\text{ MB}$, a $\RAMReductionMinRatio\times$ to $\RAMReductionMaxRatio\times$ memory reduction for PicoClaw). Lifetime peak physical footprint (\texttt{proc\_peak\_phys\_mb}) confirms PicoClaw requires only $\PicoPeakPhysMean\text{ MB}$ vs. $\OpenMinPeakPhysMean\text{ MB}$ for Node.js V8 ($\PeakPhysLeanerRatio\times$ leaner).
    \item \textbf{Tool Catalog Ablation and EDP Efficiency (Dual-Regime Dynamics):} Evaluating 54 enterprise schemas (OpenClaw Full) against 15 schemas (Minimal) reveals severe prompt bloat: W1 mean latency balloons from $\WOneOpenMinMean\text{ ms}$ to $\WOneOpenFullMean\text{ ms}$, while mean energy escalates from $\WOneOpenMinEnergy\text{ J}$ to $\WOneOpenFullEnergy\text{ J}$ ($\WOneEnergyBloatRatio\times$). In the 10-turn autonomous ReAct loop (W4) under Regime A, OpenClaw Full expends $\WFourOpenFullEnergy\text{ J}$ with an $\text{EDP}$ of $\WFourOpenFullEDP\text{ J}\cdot\text{s}$ ($\WFourOpenFullMedian\text{ ms}$ median), whereas PicoClaw completes W4 in $\WFourPicoMedian\text{ ms}$ median with an $\text{EDP}$ of $\WFourPicoEDP\text{ J}\cdot\text{s}$ ($\WFourEDPReductionPct\%$ lower EDP), demonstrating significant stochastic effect sizes ($d = \WFourCliffsDelta$, $\hat{A}_{12} = \WFourAOneTwo$, adjusted $p \WFourAdjP$) by avoiding 54-tool catalog prompt bloat. Under Regime B with amortized prefill, equalized arms converge in EDP ($944.72\text{ J}\cdot\text{s}$ for PicoClaw vs. $943.48\text{ J}\cdot\text{s}$ for OpenClaw Minimal), while remaining $43.1\%$ lower than OpenClaw Full ($1661.07\text{ J}\cdot\text{s}$).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Execution Instructions}
Reviewers can reproduce the entire analysis without local model weights or superuser privileges via:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=bash]
# Fast reviewer portable dry-run (multi-OS, under 5 seconds):
make dry-run

# Automated unit testing and linting:
make test

# Physical benchmark execution in standard user space:
make benchmark
\end{lstlisting}

\section{Impact}
\label{sec:impact}
This benchmark suite provides the research and software engineering communities with a reproducible, modular testbed to isolate and quantify AI agent orchestration runtime overhead. By decoupling orchestrator dispatch and schema validation delays from neural model latency, the suite enables systems researchers to systematically evaluate language runtime trade-offs (compiled Go, JIT Node.js, and CPython) under realistic tool-use loops \cite{yao2023react,schick2023toolformer,lewis2020retrieval}. The framework's modular adapter architecture facilitates adding new agent runtimes or language environments (e.g., Rust, Zig, or Julia) with fewer than 40 lines of code. Furthermore, the dual-tier telemetry approach---combining unprivileged kernel performance counters with non-intrusive Apple SMC and Linux RAPL electrical shunt sampling---provides an extensible blueprint for benchmarking edge AI systems under strict power and thermal budgets \cite{georgiou2022energy,you2023zeus,barroso2007case}.

\section{Conclusions}
\label{sec:conclusions}
The \texttt{picoclaw-vs-openclaw-benchmark} framework delivers an open, extensible methodology for systems-level evaluation of AI agent orchestrators on unified memory microarchitectures. Our empirical findings demonstrate that under equalized tool schemas and equivalent generation token counts, end-to-end task latency across runtimes exhibits near-parity ($< 6\%$ variance in W1 and W3) as package power saturates near 16~W on the Metal GPU. However, native compiled Go execution provides decisive microarchitectural advantages over managed V8 JIT and CPython runtimes: executing $3.83\times$ fewer retired CPU instructions, consuming $4.64\times$ fewer CPU cycles, and maintaining a $2.18\times$ to $7.61\times$ lower physical DRAM footprint. Furthermore, controlled ablation characterizes how stock enterprise tool catalog bloat severely penalizes latency through super-linear scaling compatible with transformer attention complexity rather than orchestrator dispatch delay, which can be mitigated effectively via KV-cache reuse. Through standardized workload tiers, rigorous non-parametric statistics, and portable reviewer dry-runs, this software establishes a foundation for evaluating next-generation edge intelligence.

\section*{Acknowledgements}
This work was conducted at the Center for Engineering, Modeling and Applied Social Sciences (CECS) of the Federal University of ABC (UFABC), Brazil.

\section*{Data Availability}
The complete raw experimental dataset (including \texttt{raw\_trials.jsonl}, \texttt{prefill\_ablation.jsonl}, and \texttt{warm\_cache\_trials.jsonl}), cryptographic SHA-256 verification manifests, statistical hypothesis test matrices, and automated chart generation scripts are openly available in the repository under the Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International (CC BY 4.0) license at \url{https://github.com/rafapcassetta/picoclaw-vs-openclaw-benchmark} and permanently preserved on Zenodo (\url{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.PENDING_RELEASE}) in accordance with software citation principles \cite{smith2016software}. To comply with third-party model licensing and platform portability, proprietary model weights (\texttt{models/}) and pre-compiled node/go dependencies (\texttt{tools/}) are not redistributed in the repository; they are deterministically downloaded or compiled via \texttt{scripts/fetch\_testbed\_assets.sh}. Across all experimental campaigns and ablation runs conducted on the Apple Silicon M4 hardware testbed ($1{,}320$ benchmark trials + $528$ prefill ablation runs), total electrical energy consumed was approximately $580\text{ kJ}$ ($\approx 0.16\text{ kWh}$).

# Cover Letter for Submission to SoftwareX (Elsevier)

**To:**  
The Editor-in-Chief  
*SoftwareX*, Elsevier  

**Subject:** Submission of Original Software Article:  
*"PicoClaw vs. OpenClaw: An Extensible, Multi-Platform Benchmark Suite for Autonomous AI Agent Orchestration Architectures on Apple Silicon"*

**Author:**  
Rafael Paniccia Cassetta  
Centro de Engenharia, Modelagem e Ciências Sociais Aplicadas (CECS)  
Universidade Federal do ABC (UFABC), Santo André, SP, Brazil  
Email: `rafael.paniccia@aluno.ufabc.edu.br`  

---

Dear Editor-in-Chief and Editorial Board of *SoftwareX*,

I am pleased to submit our original software manuscript titled **"PicoClaw vs. OpenClaw: An Extensible, Multi-Platform Benchmark Suite for Autonomous AI Agent Orchestration Architectures on Apple Silicon"** for consideration as an original research software article in *SoftwareX*.

### Originality and Scientific Contribution
With the rapid migration of autonomous AI agents from remote cloud endpoints to local client devices, execution runtime bottlenecks have become a defining constraint. On unified memory architectures such as Apple Silicon (UMA @ 120 GB/s), agent orchestrators compete directly with quantized neural weights (`llama.cpp`) for memory bus bandwidth and compute cycles.

Our software, `picoclaw-vs-openclaw-benchmark`, addresses this fundamental challenge by providing:
1. **Mathematical Isolation of Orchestrator Overhead ($T_{\text{orch}}$):** A modular framework decoupling neural inference latency from agent execution delay across four standardized workload tiers (W1–W4).
2. **Two-Layer Hardware Telemetry and Privilege Model:** Dual-layer physical instrumentation combining (i) system-wide package electrical power sampling via Apple SMC (`powermetrics` at 50 ms resolution, numerically integrated via the composite trapezoidal rule) with (ii) per-process microarchitectural attribution (`ri_instructions`, `ri_cycles`, `ri_billed_energy`, and `ri_lifetime_max_phys_footprint`) polled from the Darwin XNU kernel via `libproc` (`proc_pid_rusage` with `RUSAGE_INFO_V4`). Crucially, privilege isolation is strictly enforced: all agent runtimes, orchestrator child processes, and the `llama-server` daemon execute exclusively in unprivileged user space, with graceful non-root degradation marking electrical fields as `N/A` without synthetic proxies.
3. **Rigorous Non-Parametric Statistics:** Full automated computation of Energy-Delay Product ($\text{EDP} = E \times T$), Medians and Interquartile Ranges ($\text{IQR}$), two-sided Mann-Whitney $U$ tests adjusted via the step-down Holm-Bonferroni method, Cliff's Delta ($d$), and Vargha-Delaney effect sizes ($\hat{A}_{12}$) with empirical 95% bootstrap confidence intervals (2,000 resamples).
4. **Controlled Ablation Matrix and Empirical Findings:** Physical evaluation across 600 trials ($n=30$ per condition) demonstrates that the native Go runtime (PicoClaw) achieves complete stochastic dominance ($d = -1.0$, $\hat{A}_{12} = 0.0$, $p < 10^{-5}$) in long autonomous ReAct loops (W4), delivering **50.98% lower Energy-Delay Product**, executing **$3.78\times$ fewer instructions**, and operating with a **$4.15\times$ to $7.62\times$ leaner memory footprint** compared to enterprise Node.js/V8 configurations (OpenClaw Full).

### Multi-OS Reviewer Reproducibility
Recognizing that journal reviewers operate across diverse environments (including Linux x86_64 machines without Apple Metal GPUs or superuser access), our framework incorporates a **reviewer-friendly portable dry-run pipeline (`make dry-run`)**. This command dispatches a self-contained loopback mock server, executes the complete factorial matrix, runs non-parametric hypothesis tests, and renders high-resolution publication figures in under **5 seconds**, backed by continuous integration testing on both `ubuntu-latest` and `macos-latest` GitHub Actions runners.

The complete code, datasets, telemetry logs, and documentation are available under open licenses (MIT License for software and Creative Commons CC-BY 4.0 for data) at:  
👉 `https://github.com/drsigma/picoclaw-vs-openclaw-benchmark`  
Permanent Zenodo archive DOI: `10.5281/zenodo.placeholder` (prepared for Release `v1.0.0`).

This manuscript has not been published elsewhere and is not currently under consideration by any other journal. All authors have approved the submission.

Thank you very much for your time and consideration.

Sincerely,

**Rafael Paniccia Cassetta**  
Federal University of ABC (UFABC), Brazil  
`rafael.paniccia@aluno.ufabc.edu.br`

# Research Highlights (SoftwareX Editorial Submission)

* Empirical systems benchmark of Go and Node.js AI agent runtimes on Apple M4.
* PicoClaw Go runtime slashes peak physical RAM by 2.9x over Node.js V8.
* Hardware counters reveal 3.8x fewer instructions and 4.5x fewer CPU cycles.
* PicoClaw reduces Energy-Delay Product by 51% in 10-turn autonomous ReAct loops.
* Two-layer telemetry combines SMC power integration with per-process libproc.

# A Reproducible Benchmark Suite for Isolating Orchestration Overhead and Schema Prefill Costs in Local LLM Agents on Apple Silicon

[![CI](https://github.com/drsigma/picoclaw-vs-openclaw-benchmark/actions/workflows/ci.yml/badge.svg)](https://github.com/drsigma/picoclaw-vs-openclaw-benchmark/actions)
